Critical

Attackers Exploit Arista VeloCloud Orchestrator Command Injection Flaw

The Hacker News
2026-07-28 8 use cases 3 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

A maximum-severity security flaw impacting on-premises versions of Arista VeloCloud Orchestrator (VCO) has come under active exploitation in the wild. The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-16812 (CVSS score: 10.0), is a case of operating system command injection that could pave the way for arbitrary code execution. Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Inbound connections from Arista-attributed VeloCloud Orchestrator attacker IPs (CVE-2026-16812) · Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Outbound beacon/callback from internal host to VeloCloud Orchestrator attacker IPs (CVE-2026-16812) · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Inbound connections from Arista-attributed VeloCloud Orchestrator attacker IPs (CVE-2026-16812) AIDelivery Medium
Hunts inbound network sessions originating from the three source IPs Arista named as responsible for exploiting the CVE-2026-16812 command-injection zero-day against on-prem VeloCloud Orchestrator. Because these are vendor-attributed attacker IPs targeting an internet-exposed management plane, any inbound hit against orchestrator or perimeter infrastructure is a high-fidelity exploitation attempt. Rationale: Uses the three source IPs (8.19.75.217, 206.72.242.124, 206.72.242.162) Arista published as 'conducting the attacks' against on-prem VCO; vendor-attributed exploitation infrastructure targeting an internet-exposed orchestrator is a hard IOC with near-zero benign inbound rate. Cross-checked against: • https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/alerts/2026/07/27/cisa-adds-two-known-exploited-vulnerabilities-catalog • https://www.securityweek.com/critical-arista-velocloud-orchestrator-vulnerability-exploited-as-zero-day/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/arista-patches-velocloud-orchestrator-zero-day-exploited-in-attacks/
ATT&CKT1190 T1059
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate external vulnerability scanners or CTI providers that happen to reuse hosting IP space if an address is later reassigned
  • Internet background-noise/mass-scanning services (Shodan, Censys) reaching the perimeter from an overlapping range
  • An internal proxy/NAT aggregating traffic that makes the attacker IP appear against many internal hosts
  • IP later recycled by shared-hosting to a benign SaaS the org legitimately uses
  • Threat-intel enrichment sandboxes replaying the IOC IP for validation
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm which perimeter/orchestrator asset the SourceIP hit, pull the matching web/access and backend application logs around that timestamp, and check whether the destination is a VeloCloud Orchestrator on-prem instance. 2) Contain: block all three IPs at the firewall, isolate the VCO host if the connection reached it, and preserve VCO web access logs, backend/app logs, system logs, DB logs and file-system timestamps before remediation. 3) Hunt: pivot to outbound connections from the same host to these IPs (beaconing), review recent VCO administrator activity for unexpected changes, and validate managed VeloCloud Edge device state/credentials since compromise can pivot to Edges.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteIP in ("8.19.75.217","206.72.242.124","206.72.242.162")
| where ActionType in ("InboundConnectionAccepted","ConnectionRequest","ListeningConnectionCreated")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, LocalIP, LocalPort, RemoteIP, RemotePort, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where SourceIP in ("8.19.75.217","206.72.242.124","206.72.242.162")
| project TimeGenerated, DeviceVendor, DeviceProduct, DeviceAction, SourceIP, SourcePort, DestinationIP, DestinationPort, DestinationHostName, ApplicationProtocol, RequestURL
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Outbound beacon/callback from internal host to VeloCloud Orchestrator attacker IPs (CVE-2026-16812) AICommand & Control High
Hunts outbound connections from any internal host — especially the VeloCloud Orchestrator on-prem host — to the three IPs Arista attributed to CVE-2026-16812 exploitation. Arista explicitly warns operators to check for unexpected outbound network activity from the VCO host; an internal-to-attacker-IP session indicates post-exploitation callback/exfiltration rather than mere scanning. Rationale: Uses the same three Arista-attributed IPs but pivots on them as the destination (outbound), directly implementing Arista's guidance to 'check for unexpected outbound network activity from the VCO host'; an internal host initiating traffic to attacker infrastructure is a confirmed-compromise signal, not scan noise. Cross-checked against: • https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/alerts/2026/07/27/cisa-adds-two-known-exploited-vulnerabilities-catalog • https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/07/28/arista-patches-actively-exploited-velocloud-bug-as-cisa-puts-admins-on-the-clock/5279414 • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/arista-patches-velocloud-orchestrator-zero-day-exploited-in-attacks/
ATT&CKT1071 T1041
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security team actively probing/validating the IOC IPs from a jump box during this incident
  • A benign SaaS or CDN reassigned to the same IP after attacker infra takedown, legitimately contacted by internal hosts
  • Vulnerability-scanning appliance reaching out to the IPs as part of an external attack-surface scan
  • Egress proxy that sources all outbound traffic, making one proxy host appear as the sole talker to the IP
  • Threat-intel platform auto-resolving/pinging indicators for enrichment
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the SourceIP/DeviceName initiating the outbound session and confirm whether it is the VeloCloud Orchestrator host or a managed adjacent asset; review the initiating process and port/frequency for beacon patterns. 2) Contain: block the three IPs at egress, isolate the calling host, rotate VCO and managed VeloCloud Edge credentials, and preserve VCO web/app/system/DB logs plus file-system timestamps. 3) Hunt: search for inbound sessions from the same IPs (initial exploitation), unexpected VCO admin/config changes, new accounts or scheduled tasks on the host, and validate managed Edge device state per Arista guidance since orchestrator compromise can reach Edge devices.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteIP in ("8.19.75.217","206.72.242.124","206.72.242.162")
| where ActionType in ("ConnectionSuccess","ConnectionRequest","ConnectionFound")
| summarize FirstSeen=min(Timestamp), LastSeen=max(Timestamp), Connections=count(), Ports=make_set(RemotePort,20) by DeviceName, RemoteIP, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by LastSeen desc
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where DestinationIP in ("8.19.75.217","206.72.242.124","206.72.242.162")
| summarize FirstSeen=min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen=max(TimeGenerated), Connections=count(), Ports=make_set(DestinationPort,20) by SourceIP, DestinationIP, DeviceProduct, ApplicationProtocol
| order by LastSeen desc

ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-16812", "CVE-2025-68686", "CVE-2026-16723")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-16812", "CVE-2025-68686", "CVE-2026-16723")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
Email attachment opened from external sender Delivery High
Attachment delivery + execution by recipient — most common malware initial access.
ATT&CKT1566.001 T1204.002
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Endpoint.Processes EmailAttachmentInfo DeviceProcessEvents
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MalAttachments = EmailAttachmentInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project NetworkMessageId, RecipientEmailAddress,
              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                      "mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
| join kind=inner MalAttachments on $left.AccountUpn == $right.RecipientEmailAddress
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, AttachmentFileName, AttachmentSHA256
// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count
    from datamodel=Email.All_Email
    where All_Email.file_name!="-"
    by All_Email.src_user, All_Email.recipient, All_Email.file_name, All_Email.subject
| rename All_Email.recipient as user
| join type=inner user
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
        from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
        where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
          AND Processes.process_name IN ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
        by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
     | rename Processes.user as user]
| tstats summariesonly=false count
    from datamodel=Email.All_Email
    where All_Email.file_name!="-"
    by All_Email.src_user, All_Email.recipient, All_Email.file_name, All_Email.subject
| rename All_Email.recipient as user
| join type=inner user
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
        from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
        where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
          AND Processes.process_name IN ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
        by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
     | rename Processes.user as user]
Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process Exploitation High
Classic macro/exploit pattern — Office app launching cmd/powershell/wmic/regsvr32.
ATT&CKT1059.001 T1059.005 T1218
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```

Email attachment opened from external sender Delivery High
Attachment delivery + execution by recipient — most common malware initial access.
ATT&CKT1566.001 T1204.002
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Endpoint.Processes EmailAttachmentInfo DeviceProcessEvents
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MalAttachments = EmailAttachmentInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project NetworkMessageId, RecipientEmailAddress,
              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                      "mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
| join kind=inner MalAttachments on $left.AccountUpn == $right.RecipientEmailAddress
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, AttachmentFileName, AttachmentSHA256
// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc
ENDPOINT

Dysphoria IoT Botnet Adds Blockchain C2 and Victim Relays After JackSkid Disruption

The Hacker News
2026-07-27 13 use cases 3 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

Dysphoria, an Internet of Things (IoT) botnet line tracked by CNCERT and XLab, has adopted blockchain-based name services and infected-device relays after a March law-enforcement operation against JackSkid infrastructure. The researchers say the design makes the botnet harder to disrupt. CNCERT, China's national computer emergency response team, and XLab, the threat-intelligence lab of Chinese

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] CVE-2025-9528 Linksys E1700 /goform/systemCommand command injection · [LLM] Inbound Telnet/SSH weak-credential access to IoT/edge gear (Dysphoria vector) · Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Dysphoria process masquerade as libdalvikengine.so on Linux/IoT hosts
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Dysphoria botnet blockchain (ENS/SNS) name-service C2 resolution · [LLM] Dysphoria distribution-node egress + /nodes?key=meowmeowmeow server-list fetch · [LLM] Dysphoria victim-relay mesh heartbeat to *.trees4sale.net / peer domains on port 9000 · Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Dysphoria botnet blockchain (ENS/SNS) name-service C2 resolution AICommand & Control High
Hunts endpoints resolving the specific Ethereum Name Service and Solana Name Service domains Dysphoria uses to conceal C2 (burrberry.eth, ukranianhorseriding.eth, m3rnbvs5d.eth, 24carnforth2merseyside.sol). These attacker-registered blockchain names are the campaign's headline resilience mechanism and are near-unique to it, so any resolution is high-signal. Rationale: Uses the four verbatim ENS/SNS C2 domains cross-confirmed by both The Hacker News and XLab's technical write-up; blockchain-name resolution from an enterprise host is virtually never benign, making this a rare, high-fidelity signal. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.xlab.qianxin.com/botnet-rising-star-the-evolution-and-in-depth-technical-analysis-of-dysphoria/ • https://securityaffairs.com/196182/malware/dysphoria-botnet-uses-blockchain-domains-to-hide-c2-infrastructure.html • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/new-dysphoria-ddos-botnet-spreads-to-200k-devices-worldwide/
ATT&CKT1568 T1071.001
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Blockchain developer workstation resolving *.eth/*.sol via ENS gateways (eth.limo/eth.link) for legitimate Web3 work
  • Security researcher or threat-intel analyst deliberately resolving the IOC domains during triage
  • Browser extensions (MetaMask, Phantom) performing ENS/SNS lookups on a corporate laptop
  • A DNS sinkhole or passive-DNS collector re-resolving submitted indicators
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("metamask.exe","phantom.exe","brave.exe","chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe")
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the resolving host and process; confirm it is IoT/edge/server gear with no business reason to touch ENS/SNS. Pull the resolved IP(s) and check for follow-on connections to port 9000. 2) Contain: block the four domains and any resolved IPs at egress/DNS; isolate the device if it is an unmanaged IoT/router. 3) Hunt: pivot on the resolving host for the /nodes?key=meowmeowmeow fetch, port-9000 heartbeats to *.trees4sale.net, and inbound Telnet/SSH that preceded the resolution.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteUrl has_any ("burrberry.eth","ukranianhorseriding.eth","m3rnbvs5d.eth","24carnforth2merseyside.sol")
| summarize FirstSeen=min(Timestamp), LastSeen=max(Timestamp), Count=count(), RemoteIPs=make_set(RemoteIP,20) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, RemoteUrl
| order by LastSeen desc
DnsEvents
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where Name has_any ("burrberry.eth","ukranianhorseriding.eth","m3rnbvs5d.eth","24carnforth2merseyside.sol")
| summarize FirstSeen=min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen=max(TimeGenerated), Count=count(), ResolvedIPs=make_set(Result,20) by Computer, ClientIP, Name
| order by LastSeen desc

Dysphoria distribution-node egress + /nodes?key=meowmeowmeow server-list fetch AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts hosts connecting to Dysphoria distribution/C2 node IPs or fetching the hard-coded relay server list at http://<node>:9000/nodes?key=meowmeowmeow. The distinctive URI path, static key 'meowmeowmeow', and port 9000 pin this to the botnet's relay-distribution mechanism rather than a generic beacon. Rationale: Combines the article's provided distribution IPs with XLab's confirmed C2/download IPs and the verbatim relay-fetch URI '/nodes?key=meowmeowmeow' on port 9000; the static 'meowmeowmeow' key and path make the L7 match essentially FP-free. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.xlab.qianxin.com/botnet-rising-star-the-evolution-and-in-depth-technical-analysis-of-dysphoria/ • https://iottechnews.com/news/dysphoria-iot-botnet-uses-blockchain-domains-to-hide-200k-bots/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1102
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate internal application coincidentally using TCP/9000 (SonarQube, some Portainer/monitoring agents) — filtered by requiring the /nodes URI or the IOC IP set
  • Threat-intel/EDR appliance actively probing the IOC IPs for enrichment
  • Shared-hosting IP reuse: one of the relay IPs later re-assigned to a benign service
  • Vulnerability scanner reaching port 9000 during an authorized sweep
Suppression filter
| where not (RemotePort == 9000 and RemoteIP !in ("194.87.198.208","194.87.198.104","194.58.38.79","194.58.38.49","194.58.38.96","144.31.38.215","217.60.195.160","76.164.203.171","92.42.100.131","78.153.155.152") and RemoteUrl !has "/nodes" and RemoteUrl !has "meowmeowmeow")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm whether the hit was the IOC IP list or the /nodes?key=meowmeowmeow URI (URI = near-certain TP). Capture the returned node list — those IPs become new blocking indicators. 2) Contain: block the IOC IPs and port-9000 egress from the host; isolate the device. 3) Hunt: on the same host look for prior ENS/SNS resolution (UC1), inbound Telnet/SSH access, and DDoS-module behaviour (high outbound packet rates to varied destinations).

| order by Timestamp desc

CVE-2025-9528 Linksys E1700 /goform/systemCommand command injection AIExploitation Medium
Hunts HTTP requests to the Linksys E1700 systemCommand endpoint (/goform/systemCommand) that Dysphoria weaponises as one of its IoT RCE propagation flaws. Surfaces both internal hosts scanning/exploiting other routers and inbound exploitation captured by a monitored proxy/WAF. Rationale: Keys on the exact vulnerable path '/goform/systemCommand' documented for CVE-2025-9528 (Linksys E1700 systemCommand function), confirmed via NVD and the GitHub advisory; this URI has no legitimate use outside that specific router firmware. Cross-checked against: • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-9528 • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-3m47-gr9p-qrv3 • https://www.tenable.com/cve/CVE-2025-9528
ATT&CKT1190 T1059
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Authorized vulnerability scan (Nessus/Qualys) probing for CVE-2025-9528 across the router estate
  • Internet-wide research scanners (Censys/Shodan/GreyNoise) hitting an exposed Linksys device
  • A network admin legitimately administering an E1700 via its systemCommand form
  • Security team replaying the public PoC during patch validation
Suppression filter
| where SourceIP !in ("<authorized-scanner-ip>") | where RequestURL matches regex @"(?i)/goform/systemCommand.*[;&|`$]"
Response runbook
1) Triage: determine direction — is an internal host issuing the request (compromised, now scanning) or is it inbound to an exposed E1700? Check the query string for injected shell metacharacters (;, |, $(). 2) Contain: for inbound, block the source and take the router offline/patch; for outbound, isolate the internal source host. 3) Hunt: if a router was hit, watch it for subsequent Telnet/SSH logins, ENS/SNS resolution (UC1), and port-9000 node fetches (UC2) indicating successful infection.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteUrl has "/goform/systemCommand"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl
| order by Timestamp desc
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where RequestURL has "/goform/systemCommand"
| project TimeGenerated, DeviceVendor, SourceIP, DestinationIP, DestinationPort, RequestMethod, RequestURL, RequestClientApplication, DeviceAction
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Inbound Telnet/SSH weak-credential access to IoT/edge gear (Dysphoria vector) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts inbound Telnet (23) and SSH (22) authentication against IoT/edge devices — Dysphoria's most consistent propagation vector per CNCERT/XLab — with high-confidence enrichment when the source is a known Dysphoria distribution/C2 IP. Prioritises successful or high-volume attempts from public sources. Rationale: Article and XLab name Telnet/SSH weak-password guessing as the most consistent Dysphoria entry vector; the query stays high-fidelity by anchoring on the confirmed IOC IP set for alerting while a volume threshold catches unattributed brute-force against port 22/23. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.xlab.qianxin.com/botnet-rising-star-the-evolution-and-in-depth-technical-analysis-of-dysphoria/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/new-dysphoria-ddos-botnet-spreads-to-200k-devices-worldwide/
ATT&CKT1110.001 T1078.001
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Authentication.Authentication
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate remote administration of network gear over SSH from a jump host
  • Authorized security scanner performing credential-audit checks against SSH/Telnet
  • Automation/backup jobs (Ansible, rsync-over-SSH) generating high connection counts
  • Monitoring probes repeatedly opening TCP/22 for liveness checks
Suppression filter
| where SourceIP !in ("<jump-host-ip>","<backup-automation-ip>","<authorized-scanner-ip>")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm whether any attempt succeeded (Accepted password / LogonSuccess) and whether the source is in the Dysphoria IOC set. Telnet (23) success on IoT is inherently suspicious. 2) Contain: block the source IP, force-rotate credentials on the targeted device, and disable Telnet/remote management if unneeded (per the advisory's remediation). 3) Hunt: on any device with a successful login, pivot to UC1/UC2/UC5 (blockchain resolution, node fetches, relay heartbeats) to confirm infection.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType == "InboundConnectionAccepted"
| where LocalPort in (22, 23) and RemoteIPType == "Public"
| extend KnownDysphoriaSrc = RemoteIP in ("194.87.198.208","194.87.198.104","194.58.38.79","194.58.38.49","194.58.38.96","144.31.38.215","217.60.195.160","76.164.203.171","92.42.100.131","78.153.155.152")
| summarize FirstSeen=min(Timestamp), LastSeen=max(Timestamp), Attempts=count() by DeviceName, LocalPort, RemoteIP, KnownDysphoriaSrc
| where Attempts > 20 or KnownDysphoriaSrc == true
| order by LastSeen desc
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where DestinationPort in (22, 23)
| extend KnownDysphoriaSrc = SourceIP in ("194.87.198.208","194.87.198.104","194.58.38.79","194.58.38.49","194.58.38.96","144.31.38.215","217.60.195.160","76.164.203.171","92.42.100.131","78.153.155.152")
| summarize FirstSeen=min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen=max(TimeGenerated), Attempts=count(), Targets=dcount(DestinationIP) by SourceIP, DestinationPort, KnownDysphoriaSrc
| where Attempts > 20 or KnownDysphoriaSrc == true
| order by Attempts desc

Dysphoria victim-relay mesh heartbeat to *.trees4sale.net / peer domains on port 9000 AICommand & Control High
Hunts compromised devices registering into Dysphoria's victim-relay mesh by beaconing to its coordination/heartbeat infrastructure (login.trees4sale.net, *.peer4you.net, *.saintpetersburgresident.ru, dysphoria.androiddebugbridge.su), often over port 9000. This is the 'victim relay' resilience layer that forwards traffic to keep real controllers hidden. Rationale: Targets the relay-mesh coordination domains and port-9000 heartbeat endpoint (login.trees4sale.net:9000, peer4you/saintpetersburg peer domains) documented verbatim in XLab's analysis; these are attacker-registered names with no legitimate enterprise use, and this pivot is distinct from the blockchain-resolution and distribution-IP UCs. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.xlab.qianxin.com/botnet-rising-star-the-evolution-and-in-depth-technical-analysis-of-dysphoria/ • https://securityaffairs.com/196182/malware/dysphoria-botnet-uses-blockchain-domains-to-hide-c2-infrastructure.html
ATT&CKT1090.001 T1571
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Threat-intel platform re-resolving the submitted relay domains during enrichment
  • Passive-DNS or sandbox detonation environment contacting the domains
  • Typosquat collision where a benign new service registers a similar-looking hostname
  • Analyst manually browsing to the IOC domain during investigation
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the beaconing host and whether traffic is periodic/JSON-shaped over port 9000 (relay heartbeat). Confirm the device is acting as a forwarder (symmetric in/out traffic). 2) Contain: sinkhole/block the relay domains and port-9000 egress; isolate the device — a relay may be bridging external nodes into the internal network. 3) Hunt: enumerate other internal hosts resolving the same domains, check for UPnP port-mapping bursts on the local gateway and inbound connections on many high ports consistent with relay operation.

| order by LastSeen desc

| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| sort - lastTime
Dysphoria process masquerade as libdalvikengine.so on Linux/IoT hosts AIInstallation Low
Hunts Linux/edge hosts where a running process tampers with its name to masquerade as 'libdalvikengine.so' — a shared-object filename abused by Dysphoria samples as a process name to blend in. A .so name used as an executable process is anomalous and specific to this family. Rationale: Uses the verbatim masquerade string 'libdalvikengine.so' from XLab's technical analysis; a shared-object filename executing as a process is a strong anomaly, though coverage is limited to Linux/edge hosts running an EDR agent. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.xlab.qianxin.com/botnet-rising-star-the-evolution-and-in-depth-technical-analysis-of-dysphoria/
ATT&CKT1036.004 T1204.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate Android/Dalvik-related toolchain or emulator on a developer Linux host referencing libdalvikengine.so
  • Antivirus/EDR quarantine log echoing the filename in SyslogMessage during a detection
  • Security researcher analysing the sample in a monitored lab VM
  • A backup or file-integrity tool listing the .so path in its process arguments
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("clamscan","clamd","freshclam","mdatp")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the process is executing (not merely a library load or a log echo); capture its full path, parent, hash, and open sockets. 2) Contain: kill the process, isolate the host, and preserve the binary for analysis; block associated C2 IPs/domains from UC1/UC2/UC5. 3) Hunt: sweep other Linux/IoT hosts for the same process name, correlate with blockchain-domain resolution and port-9000 heartbeats, and review inbound Telnet/SSH that preceded first execution.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "libdalvikengine.so" or ProcessCommandLine has "libdalvikengine.so"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
Syslog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where SyslogMessage has "libdalvikengine.so"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Facility, ProcessName, SyslogMessage
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"

    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")

     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]

              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;

    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)

    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")

              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;

   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"

Critical

Public Exploit Released for Patched vBulletin Pre-Auth Code Execution Flaw

The Hacker News
2026-07-27 8 use cases 3 techniques 4 kill-chain phases detected

Public exploit details released on July 27 show how an unauthenticated request can reach PHP's eval() function inside vBulletin and execute code on an unpatched forum server. The attack requires no account, administrative access, or interaction from another user. SSD Secure Disclosure lists vBulletin 6.2.1 and earlier, and 6.1.6 and earlier, as affected, but does not give a lower version

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → [LLM] Internet-facing hosts still exposed to vBulletin CVE-2026-61511 (unpatched < 6.2.2) · Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] vBulletin CVE-2026-61511 pre-auth RCE: POST to ajax/render/pagenav template route · Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] vBulletin web-server process (php-fpm/httpd/nginx) spawning shell after CVE-2026-61511 RCE
vBulletin CVE-2026-61511 pre-auth RCE: POST to ajax/render/pagenav template route AIExploitation High
Hunts unauthenticated exploitation of vBulletin CVE-2026-61511 where an attacker POSTs a crafted pagenav[pagenumber] value to the public 'ajax/render/pagenav' route, driving vB5_Template_Runtime::runMaths() into PHP eval(). Article-specific because it keys on the exact vulnerable template route plus the 'phpfuck' digit/XOR/parenthesis-only payload shape rather than a generic web-attack signature. Rationale: Uses the exact vulnerable route 'ajax/render/pagenav' and parameter 'pagenav[pagenumber]' named in the SSD advisory, plus POST method and phpfuck marker chars (^ / ~ / nested parens) that separate exploit traffic from legit numeric pagination. Cross-checked SSD advisory, BleepingComputer, and KarmaInSecurity KIS-2026-13. Cross-checked against: • https://ssd-disclosure.com/vbulletin-runtime-template-runmaths-preauth-rce/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/vbulletin-fixes-critical-pre-auth-rce-flaw-with-public-exploit/ • https://karmainsecurity.com/KIS-2026-13 • https://gbhackers.com/vbulletin-pre-auth-rce-flaw/
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate AJAX pagination hitting ajax/render/pagenav via GET with a plain numeric pagenumber
  • Web vulnerability scanners (Nessus/Qualys/Nuclei) probing the CVE-2026-61511 route in authorized scans
  • Internal security team running the public SSD PoC during patch validation
  • Uptime/synthetic monitors that replay forum navigation requests
  • WAF in monitor-only mode logging blocked exploit attempts that never reached the app
Suppression filter
| where not (RequestMethod =~ "GET" and RequestURL matches regex @"pagenumber(%5D)?=\d{1,4}(&|$)")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full request for the flagged SourceIP - confirm method=POST and inspect the pagenav[pagenumber] body for digit/XOR/paren-only content (phpfuck) versus a small integer. Note the responding host's vBulletin version. 2) Contain: block the SourceIP at the WAF/edge; if the host is <=6.2.1 (6.x) or <=5.7.5 (5.x) pull it behind auth or offline until upgraded to 6.2.2 / branch patch. 3) Hunt: pivot to UC2 (web-server process spawning a shell) on the same host/time window; check for new files under the vBulletin webroot, outbound connections from php-fpm/httpd, and cron/systemd persistence.
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where isnotempty(RequestURL)
| where RequestURL has "ajax/render/pagenav"
// exploit is a POST that carries the phpfuck body; legit AJAX pagination is a GET with a small numeric pagenumber
| where RequestMethod =~ "POST" or RequestURL has_any ("pagenav%5Bpagenumber%5D","^","~","%5E","%7E")
| project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, DestinationHostName, RequestMethod, RequestURL, RequestClientApplication, DeviceProduct
| order by TimeGenerated desc

vBulletin web-server process (php-fpm/httpd/nginx) spawning shell after CVE-2026-61511 RCE AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts post-exploitation of CVE-2026-61511 where PHP system() executed via runMaths() causes the web-server worker (php-fpm, httpd, apache2, nginx) to spawn an interactive shell or recon binary. Article-specific because the exploit's whole point is arbitrary OS command execution as the web-server user through the pagenav template. Rationale: Keys on the specific parent set of vBulletin web-server workers (php-fpm/httpd/apache2/nginx, plus w3wp/php-cgi on IIS) spawning shells/recon binaries - the direct observable of PHP system() being reached through runMaths() eval() per the SSD write-up. Parent+child pairing keeps FPs low. Cross-checked SSD advisory and CybersecurityNews. Cross-checked against: • https://ssd-disclosure.com/vbulletin-runtime-template-runmaths-preauth-rce/ • https://thehackernews.com/2026/07/public-exploit-released-for-patched.html • https://cybersecuritynews.com/vbulletin-flaw-execute-php-code/
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate vBulletin/plugin features shelling out (e.g. image processing calling convert/ffmpeg, mail via sendmail)
  • Sysadmin running maintenance scripts under the web-server user
  • Backup/monitoring agents launched from an apache-owned cron
  • Composer/deployment tasks that invoke curl or git as the web user during a release
  • Security team validating the PoC on a staging forum
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("logrotate","newrelic","freshclam","certbot") or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "cron")
Response runbook
1) Triage: capture the child ProcessCommandLine and the parent InitiatingProcessCommandLine - is it interactive recon (id/whoami/uname), a payload fetch (curl/wget to an external IP), or a reverse shell (bash -i, nc -e, python pty)? Confirm the host runs vBulletin <=6.2.1 or <=5.7.5. 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the offending php-fpm/child process tree, and rotate any secrets readable by the web-server user (DB creds in config.php, API keys). 3) Hunt: correlate back to UC1 for the originating ajax/render/pagenav POST and SourceIP; enumerate new webshell files under the webroot, outbound C2 from the web-server process, and new cron/systemd/authorized_keys persistence.

| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc

Internet-facing hosts still exposed to vBulletin CVE-2026-61511 (unpatched < 6.2.2) AIReconnaissance Medium
Surfaces managed hosts where Defender/MDVM flags CVE-2026-61511 and the device is internet-facing - the exact 'self-hosted, internet-facing forums that have not updated' population the article says carries the live risk. This is an exposure-audit pivot (vulnerability management), distinct from the traffic and process detections. Rationale: Directly targets the article's stated risk concentration - unpatched (< 6.2.2) internet-facing self-hosted vBulletin - by keying on the concrete CVE id and the IsInternetFacing device attribute. Cross-checked SSD advisory and BleepingComputer, which confirm affected ranges (6.x <= 6.2.1, 5.x <= 5.7.5) and the 6.2.2 fix. Cross-checked against: • https://ssd-disclosure.com/vbulletin-runtime-template-runmaths-preauth-rce/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/vbulletin-fixes-critical-pre-auth-rce-flaw-with-public-exploit/ • https://latesthackingnews.com/2026/07/28/vbulletin-rce-vulnerability-cve-2026-61511/
ATT&CKT1190
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • MDVM has not yet catalogued the specific vBulletin build so CveId is absent (false negative, not FP)
  • Host flagged as vulnerable but already firewalled/behind auth despite IsInternetFacing=true
  • Staging/QA vBulletin instances intentionally kept on an old build
  • Version detected but the vendor branch patch was applied without a version-string bump
  • IsInternetFacing stale for a host recently moved behind a WAF
Response runbook
1) Triage: for each internet-facing hit confirm the live vBulletin version and whether the 6.2.2 upgrade or branch patch (6.2.1/6.2.0/6.1.6) has been applied. 2) Contain: prioritise internet-facing hosts for immediate patching/upgrade to 6.2.2; where patching must wait, place a WAF virtual-patch/block on POST ajax/render/pagenav and restrict admin routes. 3) Hunt: run UC1 and UC2 against each exposed host's logs for the four-week window between the late-June patch and the July 27 disclosure to check for prior exploitation.

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

n8n Sandbox Escape Lets Workflow Editors Run OS Commands as the n8n Process

The Hacker News
2026-07-27 10 use cases 5 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

n8n has patched a high-severity expression-sandbox escape that could let an authenticated workflow editor execute operating-system commands on the server running the automation platform. Security Joes found the flaw while probing n8n's February fix for CVE-2026-27577 for another bypass. The affected ranges are &lt;2.31.5 and &gt;=2.32.0,&lt;2.32.1. n8n fixed the flaw in versions 2.31.5 and

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] n8n/Node.js process spawning shell or recon utility (sandbox-escape RCE) · Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha) · PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Article-specific behavioural hunt — n8n Sandbox Escape Lets Workflow Editors Run OS Commands as the n8n Process
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] curl/wget/nc spawned by n8n/Node reaching external hosts (post-escape C2/exfil)
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] n8n/Node child command accessing N8N_ENCRYPTION_KEY or host credential stores
n8n/Node.js process spawning shell or recon utility (sandbox-escape RCE) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the direct observable of the n8n expression-sandbox escape (GHSA-gv7g-jm28-cr3m): the n8n/Node process loading child_process and executing OS commands, surfacing as a shell (sh/bash/cmd/powershell) or recon utility (whoami/id/curl) spawned as a child of node/n8n. This is exactly the parent→child chain the advisory tells defenders to hunt. Rationale: Anchored on the advisory's explicit hunt guidance ('shells, PowerShell, curl, or wget spawned as children of the n8n or Node.js process') and the child_process load in the exploit chain; parent=node/n8n + shell/recon child is the escape's only host-level footprint. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/n8n-io/n8n/security/advisories/GHSA-gv7g-jm28-cr3m • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-vpcf-gvg4-6qwr
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1059.001 T1059.003 T1203
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate n8n Execute Command node running sh/bash by design
  • Node-based CI runners or container entrypoints invoking bash/sh
  • Health-check workflows calling curl/wget to internal APIs
  • Admin debugging inside the n8n container (id/whoami)
  • Task-runner or npm lifecycle scripts spawning shell wrappers
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("n8n-nodes-base.executeCommand","/opt/n8n/healthcheck") )
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the child ProcessCommandLine and the initiating node/n8n cmdline; confirm whether an Execute Command node exists in the workflow that ran, and identify the workflow author. 2) Contain: if the command is unexpected (recon, encryption-key read, outbound tooling), isolate the host and suspend the n8n workflow-editor account that authored/last-modified the workflow. 3) Hunt: review recently created/modified workflows for arrow functions or obfuscated JS, pivot on the same host for outbound connections and N8N_ENCRYPTION_KEY access; rotate n8n credentials and the encryption key.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node","node.exe","n8n") or InitiatingProcessParentFileName in~ ("node","node.exe","n8n")
| where FileName in~ ("sh","bash","dash","zsh","ksh","csh","cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh","pwsh.exe","whoami","id","curl","wget","nc","ncat","netcat","python","python3","perl","ruby")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, ChildProcess = FileName, ChildCmd = ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc

| where ActingProcessName has_any ("node","n8n") or ParentProcessName has_any ("node","n8n")
| where TargetProcessName has_any ("/sh","/bash","/dash","/zsh","/ksh","/csh","cmd.exe","powershell.exe","/pwsh","pwsh.exe","/whoami","/id","/curl","/wget","/nc","/ncat","/netcat","/python","/perl","/ruby")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ParentProcessName, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
